(cf. Proposition
5.15
) with a discussion of the Iwasawa descent procedure at the central critical point in the scenario when
{\rm ord}_{s=\frac{k}{2}}L(f^{*},s)=1
.

We employ in Chapter
5
our general Rubin-style formula (that we prove in §
1.3.3
) to compute the leading terms of our
p
-adic
L
-functions and establish Theorem
M
. Along the way, we explain (in §
5.2.4
) that the thick Selmer complexes produces Selmer groups that extend the Bloch–Kato Selmer groups (rather than the fine Selmer groups), and thereby reflect better the extremal exceptional zero phenomena for the critical
p
-adic
L
-functions.

Chapter 1
General constructions

We begin Chapter
1
recording the basic notation and conventions that we adopt in this work. We then review various
p
-local constructions (e.g. the “
large exponential map
in §
1.1.4
and §
1.1.8
, including that for families of Galois representations; Tamagawa numbers in §
1.1.7
). We introduce Selmer complexes in §
1.2
and note for future use their
fundamental properties (including key duality statements in §
1.2.5
). We define
p
-adic height pairings on these in §
1.3
, and conclude this chapter with the proof of Rubin-style formulae in §
1.3.3
, which we shall utilize in §
5.3
when proving leading term formulae for critical
p
-adic
L
-functions.

1.1.
The large exponential map

1.1.1.
Basic notation and conventions

Let
p
be an odd prime and let
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}:=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

Fix a compatible system
\varepsilon=(\zeta_{p^{n}})_{n\geqslant 0}
of
p^{n}
th roots of unity.
For
n\geqslant 1,
set
K_{n}:=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{n}}).

Let
K_{\infty}=\underset{n\geqslant 1}{\cup}K_{n}
and
\Gamma=\operatorname{Gal}(K_{\infty}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

For any
n\geqslant 1,
set
\Gamma_{n}:=\operatorname{Gal}(K_{\infty}/K_{n})
and
G_{n}=\operatorname{Gal}(K_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
We will denote indifferently by
\chi
both the cyclotomic character
\chi\,:\,G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
and its factorisation
\chi\,:\,\Gamma\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
through
\Gamma
.
If
n=1,
we will write
\Delta:=\operatorname{Gal}(K_{1}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
instead of
G_{1}
and denote by
\omega\,:\,\Delta\rightarrow\mu_{p-1}
the restriction of the cyclotomic character
\chi
on
\Delta.

Fix a system of generators

\gamma_{n}
of
\Gamma_{n}
such that
\gamma_{n+1}=\gamma_{n}^{p}
for all
n\geqslant 1.

Fix a coefficient field
E/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and denote by
\mathcal{O}_{E}
its ring of integers. We denote by
|\,\cdot\,|
the absolute value on
E
normalised so that
|p|=p^{-[E:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]}
. Note that we have

|a|^{-1}=[\mathcal{O}_{E}:a\mathcal{O}_{E}],\qquad\forall a\in\mathcal{O}_{E}.

Let
\Lambda:=\mathcal{O}_{E}[\Delta]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\Gamma_{1}]]
denote the Iwasawa algebra of
\Gamma

over
\mathcal{O}_{E}.
We will also work with Perrin-Riou’s large Iwasawa algebra
\mathscr{H}(\Gamma).

Let
\mathscr{H}
denote the algebra of formal power series
f(z)=\underset{j=0}{\overset{\infty}{\sum}}a_{j}z^{j}
with coefficients in
E
that converge on the open unit disc. Set

\mathscr{H}(\Gamma_{1}):=\{f(\gamma_{1}-1)\mid f(z)\in\mathscr{H}\}

and

\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\Delta]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma_{1}).

The elements

\delta_{i}=\frac{1}{|\Delta|}\underset{g\in\Delta}{\sum}\omega^{-i}(g)g,\qquad 0\leqslant i\leqslant p-2.

are idempotents of
\Delta
and one has the decompositions

(1.1)

\displaystyle\Lambda=\underset{i=0}{\overset{p-2}{\oplus}}\Lambda_{\omega^{i}}\,,

\displaystyle\textrm{ where $\Lambda_{\omega^{i}}=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\Gamma_{1}]]\delta_{i}$},

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)=\underset{i=0}{\overset{p-2}{\oplus}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)_{\omega^{i}}\,,

\displaystyle\textrm{where $\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)_{\omega^{i}}=\mathscr{H}(\Gamma_{1})\delta_{i}.$}

The algebra
\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)
comes equipped with the twisting operators
\mathrm{Tw}_{j}
given as follows (sic!):

\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(f(\gamma-1)\delta_{i})=f(\chi(\gamma_{1})^{-j}\gamma_{1}-1)\delta_{i-j},\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z}.

If
M
is any
\Gamma
-module, we denote by
\mathrm{Tw}_{j}\,:\,M\rightarrow M(j)
the twist map

\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(m)=m\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes j}.
In particular, if
M
is a
\Lambda
-module,
then the twisting morphism

\mathrm{Tw}_{j}\,:\,\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}M\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}M(j)

is explicitly given by the formula

\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(f(\gamma-1)\otimes m)=\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(f(\gamma-1))\otimes\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(m)=f(\chi^{-j}(\gamma)\gamma-1)\otimes\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(m).

We denote by
\iota
the canonical involution on
\Gamma
given by
\gamma\mapsto\gamma^{-1}
and will use the same notation for the induced involutions on
\Lambda
and
\mathscr{H}(\Gamma).

If
M
is a left
\Gamma
-module, we will denote by
M^{\iota}
the structure of a right
\Gamma
-module on
M
defined by
(m,\gamma)\mapsto\iota(\gamma)m.

The ring of formal power series
\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]
will be equipped with the following structures:

•

The action of
\Gamma
defined by
\gamma(\pi)=(1+\pi)^{\chi(\gamma)},\quad\gamma\in\Gamma
.

•

The Frobenius operator
\varphi
defined by
\varphi(\pi)=(1+\pi)^{p}-1
.

•

The left inverse
\psi
of
\varphi
defined as

(1.2)

\psi(f(\pi)):=\underset{\zeta^{p}=1}{\sum}f(\zeta(1+\pi)-1).

•

The differential operator
\partial=(1+\pi)\frac{d}{d\pi}.

Recall that
\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]^{\psi=0}
is the free
\Lambda
-submodule of
\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]
generated by
(1+\pi).
The operator
\partial
is invertible on
\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]^{\psi=0}.

For any group
G
and any left
G
-module
M,
we denote by
M^{\iota}
the right
G
-module whose underlying group
is
M
and on which
G
acts by
m\cdot g=g^{-1}m.

If
G
is a finite abelian group, we denote by
X(G)
the group of characters of

G
with values in
E.
We will always assume that
E
is big enough to contain the values
of all characters of
G.
For any primitive character
\rho\in X(G)
, we denote by

e_{\rho}=\frac{1}{|G|}\underset{g\in G}{\sum}\rho^{-1}(g)g

the corresponding indempotent of
E[G]
. For any
E[G]
-module
Y
we denote
by
Y^{(\rho)}=e_{\rho}Y
its
\rho
-isotypic component, and for any map
f\,:\,X\rightarrow Y
we denote by
f^{(\rho)}

the compositum

f^{(\rho)}:X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{[e_{\rho}]}Y^{(\rho)}.

If
A
is an affinoid algebra over
E,
we set

\mathcal{X}:=\mathrm{Spm}(A)
and will often write
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
instead
A.
Set
\Lambda_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda
and
\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{X}}(\Gamma):=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma).

Let
G
be a topological group,
and let
M
be a module over a commutative ring
A
equipped with a continuous linear action of
G.

We will denote by
C^{\bullet}(G,M)
the complex of continuous cochains of
G
with coefficients in
M
and by
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(G,M)
the corresponding object of the derived category
\mathscr{D}(A)
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules. If
G
is the absolute Galois group of a local field
K,
we will write

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K,M)
instead
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(G,M).

1.1.2.
Cohomology of
p
-adic representations

A
p
-adic representation
V
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
over an affinoid
\mathcal{X}
is a finitely generated projective
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module equipped with a continuous linear action of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}.
The complex
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},V)
computes
the continuous Galois cohomology:

H^{i}(K_{n},V):=\mathbf{R}^{i}\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V).

We will also consider
the Iwasawa cohomology
H^{i}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
which can be defined as the cohomology of the complex

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V):=\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V\widehat{\otimes}\Lambda^{\iota}).

Note that
H^{i}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=0
for
i\neq 1,2.

One has a Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence:

(1.3)

H^{i}(\Gamma_{n},H^{j}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V))\,\implies\,H^{i+j}(K_{n},V)

which induces isomorphisms

H^{0}(K_{n},V)\simeq H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)^{\Gamma_{n}},\qquad H^{2}(K_{n},V)\simeq H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)_{\Gamma_{n}}

and an exact sequence

(1.4)

0\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(K_{n},V)_{\Gamma_{n}}\longrightarrow H^{1}(K_{n},V)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(K_{n},V)^{\Gamma_{n}}\longrightarrow 0.

The first map in this exact sequence is induced by the canonical projection

\mathrm{pr}_{n}\,:\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\longrightarrow H^{1}(K_{n},V).

If

V^{\prime}\times V\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}(\chi)

is an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-bilinear map, the local duality provides a pairing

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},V^{\prime})\times\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}[-2]\,.

We denote by

(\,,\,)_{n}\,:\,H^{1}(K_{n},V^{\prime})\times H^{1}(K_{n},V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}

the induced pairing on the first cohomology.

There exists the Iwasawa-theoretic analog of this pairing

(1.6)

\left<\,,\,\right>\,:\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V^{\prime})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)^{\iota}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{\mathcal{X}}

which has the following explicit description (cf.
[
PR94
]
, §3.6):

(1.7)

\left<x,y\right>\equiv\underset{\tau\in G_{n}}{\sum}\left(\tau^{-1}\mathrm{pr}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\,,\,\mathrm{pr}_{n}(y)\right)_{n}\tau\quad\mod{(\gamma_{n}-1)}\,.

From this formula it follows that for any finite character
\rho\in X(\Gamma)
of conductor
p^{n}
, we have

(1.8)

\rho\left(\left<x,y^{\iota}\right>\right)=\underset{\tau\in\Gamma/\Gamma_{n}}{\sum}\left(\tau^{-1}\mathrm{pr}_{n}^{\prime}(x),\mathrm{pr}_{n}(y)\right)_{n}\rho(\tau)=\left(e_{\rho}(x),e_{\rho}(y^{\iota})\right)_{\rho}\,,

where
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\rho}
stands for the cup-product pairing

H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V^{\prime}(\chi\rho^{-1}))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V(\rho))\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}.

We also recall the formula that describes the behavior of these pairings under cyclotomic twists.

Let
\mathscr{R}_{E}
denote the Robba ring over
E,
i.e. the ring of power series
f(\pi)=\underset{i\in\mathbb{Z}}{\sum}a_{n}\pi^{n}
with coefficients in
E
that converge on some
p
-adic annulus of the form
r(f)\leqslant\pi<1.

Define the relative Robba ring over
\mathcal{X}
as
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\mathscr{R}_{E}.
We will use freely the theory of

(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules over
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}.
If
\mathbf{D}
is a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}},
then, restricting the action of
\Gamma,
we can consider
\mathbf{D}
as a

(\varphi,\Gamma_{n})
-module. Let
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})
denote the Fontaine–Herr complex of
\mathbf{D}

in the category of
(\varphi,\Gamma_{n})
-modules. We consider
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})
as an object of
\mathscr{D}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
and set

H^{i}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}):=\mathbf{R}^{i}\Gamma(K_{n},\mathbf{D}),\qquad i\geqslant 0.

The Iwasawa cohomology
H^{i}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})
of
\mathbf{D}
is defined as the cohomology of the complex

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D}):=\left[\mathbf{D}\xrightarrow{\psi-1}\mathbf{D}\right],

concentrated in degrees
1
and
2.
Note that there exists a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{X}}(\Gamma)):

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})\simeq\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)^{\iota}).

There also exists an analogue of the spectral sequence (
1.3
) for the cohomology of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules:

H^{i}(\Gamma_{n},H^{j}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D}))\,\implies\,H^{i+j}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}).

As above, it provides us with isomorphisms

H^{0}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\simeq H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})^{\Gamma_{n}},\qquad H^{2}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\simeq H^{2}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})_{\Gamma_{n}}

and an exact sequence

(1.9)

0\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})_{\Gamma_{n}}\longrightarrow H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})^{\Gamma_{n}}\longrightarrow 0\,.

The twist
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}(\chi)
of
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}
by the cyclotomic character is a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module of rank one, and one has a canonical isomorphism

H^{2}(K_{n},\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}(\chi))\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}.

For any bilinear map

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\times\mathbf{D}\longrightarrow\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}(\chi)

the previous isomorphism induces bilinear pairings

\displaystyle\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})\times\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}[-2],

\displaystyle\left<\,,\,\right>_{\mathrm{Iw}}\,:\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D}^{\prime})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})^{\iota}\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{X}}(\Gamma),

which generalize the pairings (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) in local Galois cohomology. Note an important formula

(1.10)

\left<\mathrm{Tw}_{-j}(x),\mathrm{Tw}_{j}(y)\right>=\mathrm{Tw}_{j}\circ\left<x,y\right>.

Let
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)
be the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module associated to a
p
-adic representation
V.
Then there exists canonical
quasi-isomorphisms

(1.11)

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))\simeq\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},V),\qquad\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))\simeq\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\otimes_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathscr{H}_{\mathcal{X}}(\Gamma),

which are compatible with the pairings and spectral sequences reviewed above.

1.1.3.
Bloch–Kato exponential map

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over
E.
Set

{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}):=\left(\mathbf{D}[1/t]\right)^{\Gamma},

where
t=\log[\varepsilon]
is the “
2\pi i
” of Fontaine. The module

{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})
is equipped with a canonical Frobenius operator
\varphi
and
an exhaustive decreasing filtration
\operatorname{Fil}^{i}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}).
One says that
\mathbf{D}
is crystalline
if
\dim_{E}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})=\mathrm{rk}_{E}(\mathbf{D}).

Assume that
\mathbf{D}
is crystalline. The elements of
H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})
classify the extensions
of the trivial module
\mathscr{R}_{E}
by
\mathbf{D}
in the category of
(\varphi,\Gamma_{n})
-modules,
and for
x\in H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})
let
\mathbf{D}_{x}
denote the corresponding extension.
Define:

H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}):=\left\{x\in H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\mid{\textrm{$\mathbf{D}_{x}$ is crystalline}}\right\}

(see
[
Ben11
]
,
[
Nak14
]
).
The tangent space of
\mathbf{D}
over
K_{n}
is defined as follows:

t_{\mathbf{D}}(K_{n}):=\left({\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})/\operatorname{Fil}^{0}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}K_{n}\,.\\

In the derived category of
E
-vector spaces, consider the complex

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}):=\left[{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\xrightarrow{f}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\oplus t_{\mathbf{D}}(K_{n})\right],

where the terms are placed in degrees
0
and
1
and

f(x)=((1-\varphi)x,x\pmod{\operatorname{Fil}^{0}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\otimes K_{n}}).

There exists a canonical morphism

\mathbf{exp}_{\mathbf{D},K_{n}}\,:\,\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}),

which induces isomorphisms

\mathbf{R}^{0}\mathbf{\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\simeq H^{0}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}),\qquad\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\simeq H_{f}^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}).

This information can be encoded by the exact sequence

(1.12)

0\rightarrow H^{0}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\rightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\xrightarrow{f}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\oplus t_{\mathbf{D}}(K_{n})\xrightarrow{\exp_{\mathbf{D},K_{n}}}H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\rightarrow 0.

Here the connecting map
\exp_{\mathbf{D},K_{n}}
is the exponential map defined in
[
Ben11
]
,

[
Nak14
]
.

The dual exponential map

(1.13)

\exp^{*}_{\mathbf{D}^{*}(\chi),K_{n}}\,:\,H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}(\chi))\rightarrow\operatorname{Fil}^{0}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}(\chi))

is defined by the property

(\exp_{\mathbf{D},K_{n}}(x),y)=\operatorname{Tr}_{K_{n}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}[x,\exp^{*}_{\mathbf{D}^{*}(\chi),K_{n}}(y)],

where

[\,,\,]\,:\,({\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\otimes K_{n})\times({\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}(\chi))\otimes K_{n})\longrightarrow K_{n}

stands for the canonical duality.

If
\mathbf{D}:=\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)
is the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module associated to
a crystalline
p
-adic representation
V,
we recover the classical theory from
[
BK90
]
.
Recall that the Bloch and Kato define the tangent space
t_{V}(K_{n})
of
V
and the “finite” part of
H^{1}(K_{n},V)
as follows:

\displaystyle t_{V}(K_{n}):=\left({\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)/\operatorname{Fil}^{0}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}K_{n},

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},V):=\ker\left(H^{1}(K_{n},V)\rightarrow H^{1}(K_{n},V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbf{B}_{\textrm{cris}})\right),

where
\mathbf{B}_{\textrm{cris}}
is the ring of crystalline
p
-adic periods.
In the derived category of
E
-vector spaces, consider the complex

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},V):=\left[{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\xrightarrow{f}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\oplus t_{V}(K_{n})\right],

where the terms are placed in degrees
0
and
1
and

f(x)=((1-\varphi)x,x\pmod{\operatorname{Fil}^{0}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\otimes K_{n}}).

There exists a canonical morphism

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},V)\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},V),

which induces isomorphisms

\mathbf{R}^{0}\mathbf{\Gamma}(K_{n},V)\simeq H^{0}(K_{n},V),\qquad\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}(K_{n},V)\simeq H_{f}^{1}(K_{n},V)

(see
[
Ben14
, Section 2.1.3]
).
Note that
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\simeq{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V))
by
[
Ber08
]
. Moreover we have the following result, which also follows directly from Berger’s theory:

Proposition 1.1
.

We have the following commutative diagram with canonical morphisms:

\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)).}

In particular,

H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},V)\simeq H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)).

Proof.

See
[
Ben11
]
and
[
Ben14
]
.
∎

If
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V),
the exact sequence (
1.12
) reads

0\rightarrow H^{0}(K_{n},V)\rightarrow{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\xrightarrow{f}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\oplus t_{V}(K_{n})\xrightarrow{\exp_{V,K_{n}}}H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},V)\rightarrow 0.

Here the coboundary map
\exp_{V,K_{n}}
is the Bloch–Kato exponential map.

In particular, if
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)^{\varphi=1}=0,
one has an
isomorphism

\exp_{V,K_{n}}\,:\,t_{V}(K_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm f}(K_{n},V).

1.1.4.
The large exponential map

We review the main properties of the large exponential map in the
setting of crystalline
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules. The results of this section can be either
proved by mimicking the arguments of Berger
[
Ber03
]
, or can be extracted from the general results
of Nakamura
[
Nak14
]
, where the approach of Berger is extended to the case of general de Rham representations. However, in the general de Rham case, the relation of this construction to Iwasawa theory is not fully understood yet.

Note that, following the approach in
[
Ben14
]
, we define the large exponential map as a morphism of complexes rather than that of cohomology groups.
This point of view has technical importance since we will study Selmer complexes
with local conditions provided by some large exponential maps in Chapters
3
and
4
. The general formalism of such complexes will be recalled in Section
1.2.4
below.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a crystalline
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathscr{R}_{E}.

Set

\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D}):=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]^{\psi=0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}).

We define the maps

\widetilde{\Xi}_{\mathbf{D},n}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D})_{\Gamma_{n}}\rightarrow{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\oplus t_{\mathbf{D}}(K_{n})

by the following explicit formulae. For any
\alpha(\pi)\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D})
,

\widetilde{\Xi}_{\mathbf{D},n}(\alpha(\pi))=\begin{cases}p^{-n}\left(-\alpha(0),\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(\sigma\otimes\varphi)^{-i}\alpha(\zeta_{p^{i}}-1)\right)&\text{if $n\geqslant 1$},\\
-((1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1})\alpha(0),0)&\text{if $n=0.$}\end{cases}

These maps induce the morphisms of complexes

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{\Xi}}_{\mathbf{D},n}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D})_{\Gamma_{n}}[-1]\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{\Xi}}_{\mathbf{D},n}(\alpha(\pi))=\bigl(0,\Xi_{\mathbf{D},n}(\alpha(\pi))\mod{\operatorname{Fil}^{0}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}K_{n}}\bigr).

Assume that the following condition holds:

LE)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})^{\varphi=1}=0.

Then
\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{f}(K_{n},\mathbf{D})\simeq t_{\mathbf{D}}(K_{n})
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Xi}}_{\mathbf{D},n}
is homotopic to the map

\Xi_{\mathbf{D},n}(\alpha(\pi))=\begin{cases}p^{-n}\left(0,\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(\sigma\otimes\varphi)^{-i}\alpha(\zeta_{p^{i}}-1)+(1-\varphi)^{-1}\alpha(0)\right)&\text{if $n\geqslant 1$},\\
\displaystyle\left(0,\left(\frac{1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1}}{1-\varphi}\right)\alpha(0)\right)&\text{if $n=0.$}\end{cases}

Fix an integer
h\geqslant 1
such that
\operatorname{Fil}^{-h}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})={\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}).

Set

(1.14)

\ell_{i}=i-\frac{\log(\gamma_{1})}{\log\chi(\gamma_{1})},\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z},

where
\gamma_{1}
is the fixed generator of
\Gamma_{1}.
We remark that
\ell_{i}\in\mathscr{H}(\Gamma).

For any
\alpha(X)\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D}),

the equation

(1.15)

(\varphi-1)(F)=\alpha(\pi)-\underset{i=0}{\overset{h-1}{\sum}}\frac{\partial^{i}\alpha(0)}{i!}\log^{i}(1+\pi)

has a unique solution
F\in\mathscr{H}\otimes_{E}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})
(cf.
[
PR94
]
, Lemme 2.2.3). Let us set

\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}(\alpha):=\frac{\log\chi(\gamma_{1})}{p}\left(\underset{i=0}{\overset{h-1}{\prod}}\ell_{i}\right)\circ F(\pi).

The arguments of Berger show that

\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}(\alpha)\in\mathbf{D}^{\psi=1}.
This provides us with a map

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D})[-1]\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D}).

We will denote by

\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D})

the induced map on the first cohomology.

Theorem 1.2
.

The map
\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}
satisfies the following properties:

1)

For any
n\geqslant 0,
one has a commutative diagram

(1.16)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 30.37614pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.11804pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.93112pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.54915pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.37614pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 54.37614pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 84.37614pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}).}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

ii)

Let us denote by
d_{-1}:=\varepsilon^{-1}\otimes t
the canonical generator of

{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathscr{R}_{E}(\chi^{-1}))
. Then

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}(\chi),h+1}=-\mathrm{Tw}_{1}\circ\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}\circ(\partial\otimes d_{-1}).

iii)

One has

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h+1}=\ell_{h}\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h},

where

(1.17)

\ell_{h}=h-\frac{\log(\gamma_{1})}{\log\chi(\gamma_{1})}.

Proof.

The theorem is a reformulation of the results of Nakamura
[
Nak14
]

for the case of crystalline
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules. Note that for crystalline
p
-adic representations it follows from the results of Berger
[
Ber03
]
.
∎

Using Theorem
1.2
ii), one can
define
\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}
for all integers
h
such that
\operatorname{Fil}^{-h}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})={\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}).

Remark 1.3
.

If (
LE)
) holds, the diagram (
1.16
) induces a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 22.95966pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.11804pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
