3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 101.92595pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-19.71819pt\raise-32.58447pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{t_{\mathbf{D}}(K_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.23328pt\raise-39.20529pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62918pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(h-1)!\mathbf{exp}_{\mathbf{D},K_{n}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.71819pt\raise-32.58447pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.71819pt\raise-32.58447pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 73.71819pt\raise-32.58447pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{1}(K_{n},\mathbf{D}),}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

where
\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}
is the usual large exponential map for
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules constructed in
[
Ber03
,
Nak14
]
.

1.1.5.
Perrin-Riou’s large exponential map

If
\mathbf{D}
is the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module associated to a crystalline representation, the map
\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathbf{D},h}
coincides with Perrin-Riou’s large exponential map
\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}

[
PR94
]

(see
[
Ber03
]
). For the reader’s convenience, we record below some of the previous statements in that particular case.

Let
V
be a crystalline representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

with coefficients in
E.

Set

\mathfrak{D}(V):=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]^{\psi=0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V).

Define the maps

\widetilde{\Xi}_{V,n}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(V)\rightarrow{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\oplus t_{V}(K_{n})

by

\widetilde{\Xi}_{V,n}(\alpha(\pi))=\begin{cases}p^{-n}\left(-\alpha(0),\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(\sigma\otimes\varphi)^{-i}\alpha(\zeta_{p^{i}}-1)\right)&\text{if $n\geqslant 1$},\\
-((1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1})\alpha(0),0)&\text{if $n=0.$}\end{cases}

For any
h\geqslant 1
such that
\operatorname{Fil}^{-h}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)={\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V),
one has a
\Gamma
-equivariant map

\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(V)\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V).

satisfying the following properties:

i)

For any
n\geqslant 0,
one has a commutative diagram

(1.18)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 44.23625pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.73613pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{D}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.9207pt\raise 6.40833pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.37502pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 98.23625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.95966pt\raise-16.32pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37502pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}_{V,n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.23625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 98.23625pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 145.43636pt\raise-16.32pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 145.43636pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-44.23625pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\oplus t_{V}(K_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.08669pt\raise-26.02612pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63612pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(h-1)!\exp_{V,K_{n}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 116.7772pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.23625pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 116.7772pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{1}(K_{n},V).}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

ii)

Let us denote by
d_{-1}:=\varepsilon^{-1}\otimes t
the canonical generator of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(-1))
. Then

\mathrm{Exp}_{V(1),h+1}=-\mathrm{Tw}_{1}\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}\circ(\partial\otimes d_{-1}).

iii)

One has

(1.19)

\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h+1}=\ell_{h}\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h},

where
\ell_{h}
is given as in (
1.17
).

Recall that we set
G_{n}=\operatorname{Gal}(K_{n}/K).
Shapiro’s lemma gives an isomorphism of
E[G_{n}]
-modules

H^{1}(K_{n},V)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V\otimes_{E}E[G_{n}]^{\iota}).

On taking the
\rho
-isotypic components, we obtain isomorphisms

H^{1}(K_{n},V)^{(\rho)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V(\rho^{-1})),\qquad\qquad\rho\in X(G_{n}).

Assume that
V
satisfies (
LE)
).
Then in the diagram (
1.18
),
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Xi}}_{V,n}

can be replaced by the map

\Xi_{V,n}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(V)\longrightarrow t_{V}(K_{n})

which is defined as

\Xi_{V,n}(\alpha(\pi))=\begin{cases}p^{-n}\left(0,\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(\sigma\otimes\varphi)^{-i}\alpha(\zeta_{p^{i}}-1)+(1-\varphi)^{-1}\alpha(0)\right)&\text{if $n\geqslant 1$},\\
\displaystyle\left(0,\left(\frac{1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1}}{1-\varphi}\right)\alpha(0)\right)&\text{if $n=0.$}\end{cases}

Remark 1.4
.

Assume that
V
is the
p
-adic realization of a pure motive
\mathcal{M}/\mathbb{Q}
of motivic weight
w:=\mathrm{wt}(\mathcal{M})
which has good reduction at
p
. It follows from the definition of Hodge structures that the tangent space
t_{V}(K_{n})
is nonzero if and only if
w\leqslant-1.
On the other hand, the condition
w\leqslant-1
implies that
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)^{\varphi=1}=0
(see
[
KM74
]
, see also discussion in
[
Ben15
]
, §3.2.1). Therefore,
1-\varphi
is an isomorphism on
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V),
and the maps
\Xi_{V,n}
are well defined if the representation
V
has a motivic origin.
We also remark that the operator
1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1}
can have a nontrivial kernel and therefore the map
\Xi_{V,0}
is not necessarily injective. The non-vanishing of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)^{\varphi=p^{-1}}
is closely related to the phenomenon of exceptional zeros of
p
-adic
L
-functions attached to
\mathcal{M}.
We refer the reader to
[
Ben14
,
Ben15
]
for further details.

We record the following formula giving the
\rho
-isotypic component of the map
\Xi_{V,n}
:

Lemma 1.5
.

Assume that
\rho\in X(G_{n})
is a primitive character. Then
for any
\alpha=f(\pi)\otimes d\in\mathfrak{D}(V)

\Xi_{V,n}^{(\rho)}(\alpha)=\begin{cases}e_{\rho}(f(\zeta_{p^{n}}-1))\otimes p^{-n}\varphi^{-n}(d)&\textrm{if $n\geqslant 1$},\\
(1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1})(1-\varphi)^{-1}(d)&\textrm{if $n=0.$}\end{cases}

Proof.

See
[
BB08
, Lemma 4.10]
.
∎

We will identify
\mathfrak{D}(V)
with
\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)

using the canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[X]]^{\psi=0}\simeq\Lambda.

The duality
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{*}(1))\times{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\xrightarrow{[\,,\,]}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
extends
to a
\Lambda[1/p]
-bilinear perfect pairing

\star\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(V^{*}(1))\times\mathfrak{D}(V)\longrightarrow\Lambda[1/p].

The explicit reciprocity law for the large exponential map
(cf.
[
Ben00
,
Col98
,
Ber04
]
) states that

(1.20)

\left<\mathrm{Exp}_{V^{*}(1),1-h}(x),c\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}(y)\right>=(-1)^{h}(x\star y),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{D}(V^{*}(1)),\quad y\in\mathfrak{D}(V).

It means that the large exponential map
\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}
interpolates also the inverses of the dual
exponential maps
\exp_{V(j)}^{*}
for
j\ll 0.
We record it in the following form:

(1.21)

\mathrm{Exp}_{V^{*}(1),1-h}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{h}}{(h-1)!}(\exp_{V^{*}(1)}^{*})^{-1}((1-p^{-1}\varphi^{-1})(1-\varphi)^{-1}x(0)).

Let
\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)
denote the total ring of fractions of
\mathscr{H}(\Gamma).
Extending scalars from
\Lambda
to
\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)
, we obtain an isomorphism

\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}\,:\,\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\longrightarrow\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\,,

and its inverse

\mathrm{Log}_{V,h}\,:\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\longrightarrow\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\,.

Perrin-Riou’s approach to the construction of
p
-adic
L
-functions postulates, roughly speaking, that when
\mathbf{z}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V^{*}(1))
is the
p
-local image of the initial term of an Euler system for a global geometric Galois representation
V^{*}(1)
and
y
is a generator of a regular submodule

D
of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V),
the element
\mathrm{Log}_{V^{*}(1),h}(\mathbf{z})\star y
of
\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)
coincides with the
p
-adic
L
-function attached to the data
(V,D)
; cf.
[
PR95
,
Ben14
]
.
The explicit reciprocity law (
1.20
) can be recast in the form

\left<\mathbf{z},c\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}(y)\right>=(-1)^{h}(\mathrm{Log}_{V^{*}(1),h}(\mathbf{z})\star y),\qquad\mathbf{z}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V^{*}(1)),\quad y\in\mathscr{K}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\,,

and therefore the
p
-adic
L
-function can be alternatively constructed using the map
\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}.
In the present work, we will take the latter approach and use the map
\mathrm{Exp}_{V,h}
as it is better adapted to the study of Selmer complexes arising in Perrin-Riou’s theory.

1.1.6.
The case of one-dimensional representations

In this section, we specialize Perrin-Riou’s theory to the case of one-dimensional crystalline representations. These representations are obtained by twisting unramified characters by a power of the cyclotomic character. The large exponential map for these representations is closely related to the theory of Coleman and Yager and can be constructed on the integral level.

Let
\nu\,:\,G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{E}^{*}
be a continuous unramified character
and let
E(\nu)
denote the associated
p
-adic representation. Then
\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu)
is a canonical
lattice in
E(\nu).
The crystalline module
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(E(\nu))
contains the canonical lattice

{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu)):=(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu)\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{u}r}})^{G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}},

where
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\mathrm{u}r}}
denotes the completion of the maximal unramified extension of

\mathbb{Z}_{p}.
The Frobenius operator
\varphi
acts on
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(E(\nu))
as the multiplication by

\alpha:=\nu(\mathrm{Fr}_{p}),
where
\mathrm{Fr}_{p}
denotes the
geometric
Frobenius.

For any
j\in\mathbb{Z},
we denote by
E(\nu\chi^{j})
the
j
th cyclotomic twist of
E(\nu).

The module
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(E(\nu\chi^{j}))
has the canonical lattice

{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})):={\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu))\otimes{d[j]},

where
d[j]
denotes the canonical generator of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(j)).

Let us set

\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})):=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]^{\psi=0}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})).

There exist isomorphisms of free
\Lambda
-modules of rank one

(1.22)

\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),j}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))

which coincide with the corresponding Perrin-Riou’s exponential maps after extension of scalars
1
1

1

Here we use the fact that
\nu
is a nontrivial character.
.
Let

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),j}\,:\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))\longrightarrow\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))

denote its inverse.
We record two formulae, which follow from the general interpolation properties
of the large exponential map:

(1.23)

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{0}\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),j}(x)=(j-1)!\exp_{E(\nu\chi^{j})}\left((1-\alpha^{-1}p^{j-1})(1-\alpha p^{-j})^{-1}x\right),

\displaystyle j\geqslant 1

\displaystyle\mathrm{pr}_{0}\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),j}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{(-j)!}(\exp^{*}_{E(\nu\chi^{j})})^{-1}\left((1-\alpha^{-1}p^{j-1})(1-\alpha p^{-j})^{-1}x\right),

\displaystyle j\leqslant 0.

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
\nu\,:\,G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{E}^{*}
be a nontrivial
unramified character. Then:

For each
j\geqslant 1,
one has

\left[H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})):\exp\bigl({\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))\bigr)\right]=\left|(j-1)!\right|\cdot\left|\frac{1-\alpha^{-1}p^{j-1}}{1-\alpha p^{-j}}\right|\cdot\#H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},E/\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j})).

For each
j\leqslant 0,
one has

\left[H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})):\left(\exp^{*}\right)^{-1}\bigl({\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))\bigr)\right]=\left|(-j)!\right|^{-1}\cdot\left|\frac{1-\alpha^{-1}p^{j-1}}{1-\alpha p^{-j}}\right|\cdot\#H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},E/\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j})).

Proof.

These formulae follow immediately from (
1.23
) together with (
1.3
).
∎

Fix a generator
d
of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))
and denote by
d^{*}\in{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j}))
its dual. Then we have an integral version of the map

\mathfrak{L}_{V,1-h,d},
namely:

\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),1-j,d^{*}}\,:\,H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j}))\longrightarrow\Lambda,\qquad\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),j,d^{*}}(x):=\left<x,c\circ\mathrm{Exp}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),j}({d}\otimes(1+\pi))^{\iota}\right>.

The explicit reciprocity law says:

\mathrm{Log}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j}),1-j}(x)=(-1)^{j}\mathfrak{L}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),1-j,d^{*}}(x)d^{*},\qquad x\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j})).

1.1.7.
Tamagawa numbers

For a crystalline representation
V
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
with coefficients in
E
, consider the Bloch–Kato exact sequence

(1.24)

0\longrightarrow H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\xrightarrow{(1-\varphi,\mathrm{pr})}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)\oplus t_{V}\xrightarrow{\exp}H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\longrightarrow 0,

where
t_{V}:={\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)/{\rm Fil}^{0}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)
denotes the tangent space of
V
. Let
T\subset V
be a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-stable
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-lattice and let us put

\displaystyle L_{\rm f}(V):={\det}_{E}H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\otimes{\det}_{E}^{-1}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)

\displaystyle L_{\rm f}(T):={\det}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes{\det}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}^{-1}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\,.

The exact sequence (
1.24
) gives rise to an isomorphism

\alpha_{V}\,:\,L_{\rm f}(V)\simeq{\det}^{-1}_{E}t_{V}\,.

Let us fix a nonzero element
\omega\in{\det}_{E}\,t_{V}
. The
Tamagawa number
associated to the pair
(T,\omega)
is defined on setting

\mathrm{Tam}^{0}_{p,\omega}(T):=\left|a\right|^{-1},\qquad\textrm{where $\alpha_{V}(L_{\rm f}(T))=a\omega^{-1}$}.

If
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)^{\varphi=1}=0,
then
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)=0,
and we set

\mathrm{Tam}_{p,\omega}(T):=\left[H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T):\exp(L)\right]\,,

where
L\subset t_{V}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-lattice such that
{\det}_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}L=\mathcal{O}_{E}\omega
.
It follows directly from these definitions that

\mathrm{Tam}^{0}_{p,\omega}(T)=\bigl|{\det}_{E}(1-\varphi\,|\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V))\bigr|\cdot\mathrm{Tam}_{p,\omega}(T)\,.

Let
T=\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),
where
j\in\mathbb{Z}.
We will denote by
\mathrm{Tam}_{p,\mathrm{can}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))
the Tamagawa number
\mathrm{Tam}_{p,\omega}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})),
where

\omega
is an arbitrary generator of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})).

If
N\subset{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(E(\nu\chi^{j}))
is the
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-lattice generated
by some
\eta\in{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j})),
we set

(1.25)

\Omega_{p}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),N)=|b|,\quad\text{\rm where ${\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))=bN.$}

We remark that

(1.26)

\mathrm{Tam}^{0}_{p,\eta}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))=\mathrm{Tam}^{0}_{p,\mathrm{can}}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))\cdot\Omega_{p}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),N).

We deduce from Lemma
1.6
the following well-known formulae for the Tamagawa numbers of one-dimensional representations (cf.
[
PR94
]
):

(1.27)

\mathrm{Tam}^{0}_{p,\eta}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}))=\left|(j-1)!\right|\cdot\#H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},E/\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu^{-1}\chi^{1-j}))\cdot\Omega_{p}(\mathcal{O}_{E}(\nu\chi^{j}),N)\,.

1.1.8.
Exponential map over affinoid algebras

In this section, we record the “family-version” of the constructions of Section
1.1.6
.

For any continuous character

\delta\,:\,\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\times}
let
\mathbf{D}_{\delta}=\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}e_{\delta}
denote the
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module of rank one over
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}
, generated by
e_{\delta}
and such that

\varphi(e_{\delta})=\delta(p)e_{\delta}\quad,\quad\gamma(e_{\delta})=\delta(\chi(\gamma))e_{\delta}\qquad\qquad\gamma\in\Gamma.

For any
x\in\mathcal{X}(E)
, we denote by
\mathbf{D}_{\delta,x}
the specialization
of
\mathbf{D}_{\delta}
at
x
:

\mathbf{D}_{\delta,x}:=\mathbf{D}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},x}E.

Let
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}^{+}\subset\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}
denote the subring of analytic functions on
the open unit disc
|X|_{p}<1.
Set
\mathbf{D}_{\delta}^{+}:=\mathscr{R}_{A}^{+}e_{\delta}.

We say that
\mathbf{D}_{\delta}
is of Hodge–Tate weight
m\in\mathbb{Z}
if

\delta(u)=u^{m},\qquad\qquad\forall u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}.

If
\mathbf{D}_{\delta}
is of Hodge–Tate weight
m,
then
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta}):=\left(\mathbf{D}_{\delta}[1/t]\right)^{\Gamma}
is the free
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module of rank one generated
by
d_{\delta}=t^{-m}e_{\delta},
where
t=\log(1+\pi)
is the “
p
-adic
2\pi i
” of Fontaine.
Moreover
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta})
has the unique filtration break at
-m,
and
\varphi
acts on
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta})
as the multiplication by
p^{-m}\delta(p)
map. Set

\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta})=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[X]]^{\psi=0}\otimes_{E}{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta}).

First assume that
m\geqslant 1.
Mimicking the constructions in Section
1.1.4
, for any
\alpha(\pi)\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta})=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\pi]]^{\psi=0}

we take a solution of the equation

(\varphi-1)F=\alpha(\pi)-\underset{i=0}{\overset{h-1}{\sum}}\frac{\partial^{i}\alpha(0)}{i!}\log^{i}(1+\pi).

Then

\left(\underset{i=0}{\overset{h-1}{\prod}}\right)\ell_{i}\circ F(\pi)=(-1)^{h-1}t^{h}\partial^{h}(F)\in\left(t^{h-m}\mathbf{D}_{\delta}^{+}\right)^{\psi=1}\qquad\forall h\geqslant m.

This allows to define the map

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta},h}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta})[-1]\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbf{D}_{\delta}),\qquad\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta},h}(\alpha)=\frac{\chi(\gamma_{1})}{p}\left(\underset{i=0}{\overset{h-1}{\prod}}\ell_{i}\right)\circ F(\pi).

The following proposition is clear from construction:

Proposition 1.7
.

The following assertions hold true:

i) The map
\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta},h}
interpolates the exponentials

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta,x}}
for
x\in\mathcal{X}.

ii) For any
h\geqslant m
one has

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta},h+1}=\ell_{h}\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta},h}.

iii) For any
h\geqslant m
one has

\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta\chi},h+1}=-\mathrm{Tw}_{1}\circ\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta}}\circ(\partial\otimes d_{1}).

iv) Using property iii), one can extend the definition of
\mathbf{Exp}_{\mathbf{D}_{\delta},h}

to
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules
\mathbf{D}_{\delta}
of arbitrary Hodge–Tate weight
m\in\mathbb{Z}

and
h\geqslant m.

1.2.
Selmer complexes

1.2.1.
Local conditions

Unramified local conditions

In this section, we revisit the construction of Selmer complexes following
[
Nek06
,
Ben20
]
and introduce various Selmer groups that are utilized in this paper.

Let
p
be a fixed prime. Fix a finite set of primes
S
containing
p
and denote by
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
the Galois group of the maximal algebraic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
unramified outside
S\cup\{\infty\}
. For any topological
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
-module

M
we denote by
H^{i}(M):=H^{i}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},M)
the continuous cohomology
of
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
with coefficients in
M
.

We maintain the notation of Section
1.1
. In particular, we consider an affinoid
\mathcal{X}
over
E.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\circ}
denote the unit ball (with respect to the Gauss norm) of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
. Fix a
p
-adic representation
V
of
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
. We will assume that there exists a free
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\circ}
-lattice
T
in
V
such that
V=T[1/p]
.

Fix
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}.
Let
I_{\ell}
denote the inertia subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}.

We will work with the following versions of unramified local conditions:

•

Nekovář’s unramified local conditions.
For
M=V,T,
or
V/T,
consider the complex

C_{\textup{ur}}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},M)=\left[M^{I_{\ell}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Fr}_{\ell}-1}M^{I_{\ell}}\right],

where the terms are concentrated in degrees
0
and
1.
Let
\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)
denote the
corresponding complex in the derived categories
\mathscr{D}_{\textrm{ft}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
,

\mathscr{D}_{\textrm{ft}}(\mathcal{O}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{X}})
and
\mathscr{D}_{\textrm{coft}}(\mathcal{O}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{X}})
respectively. This complex is equipped with a natural map

g_{\ell}\,:\,C_{\textup{ur}}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},M)\longrightarrow C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},M).

One has

\mathbf{R}^{0}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\simeq H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M),\qquad\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\simeq\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\rightarrow H^{1}(I_{\ell},M)\right).

On the level of cohomology, the map
g_{\ell}
induces the isomorphism
\mathbf{R}^{0}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\simeq H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)
and the natural inclusion
\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M).

•

Bloch–Kato’s unramified local conditions
H^{1}_{\rm f}.

Here
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Spm}(E),

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}=E
and
\mathcal{O}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{E}.

Bloch–Kato’s local conditions
H^{1}_{\rm f}
are defined for the first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M):

H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M):=\begin{cases}\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)\longrightarrow H^{1}(I_{\ell},V)\right),&\textrm{if $M=V$},\\
\mathrm{im}\left(H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)\longrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V/T)\right),&\textrm{if $M=V/T$},\\
\textrm{$\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)\longrightarrow\dfrac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}{H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}\right)$},&\textrm{if $M=T$}.\end{cases}

We tautologically have
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)=\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)
. However

H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)
need not agree with
\mathbf{R}^{1}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\textup{ur}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},M)
for
M=T,V/T
: When
\mathcal{X}={\rm Spm}(E)
is a point, the difference is accounted by Tamagawa numbers.

Greenberg’s local conditions

Let
\ell=p.
We will work with Greenberg-style local conditions in the following settings:

•

Classical Greenberg’s conditions.

M=V,

T
or
V/T,
and
Z
is a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-submodule of
M.
Greenberg’s local conditions are defined as the natural map

g_{p}\,:\,C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},Z)\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},M).

On cohomology,
g_{p}
induces natural maps

H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},Z)\rightarrow H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M).

•

Greenberg’s conditions arising from
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules.
Here
M=V.
Let
C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))

denote the Fontaine–Herr complex of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V).
Recall that there exists a complex
K^{\bullet}_{p}(V)
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules
together with quasi-isomorphisms of complexes

(1.28)

C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},V)\xrightarrow{\xi_{p}}K^{\bullet}_{p}(V)\xleftarrow{\alpha_{p}}C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))\,

(cf.
[
Ben20
]
, Section 2.5). Note that these quasi-isomorphisms
induce the quasi-isomorphism (
1.11
)
between
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))
and
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V).
If
Z^{\bullet}
is a complex
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules equipped with a morphism
Z^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)),

it defines a local condition

g_{p}\,:\,Z^{\bullet}\rightarrow K^{\bullet}_{p}(V).

It gives rise to a map
Z^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
in the derived category.

Notice the following important case. Let
\mathbf{D}\subset\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)
be a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodule (not necessarily saturated). The choice of
\mathbf{D}
then defines the local condition

g_{p}\,:\,C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{\mathbf{D}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))\xrightarrow{\alpha_{p}}K^{\bullet}_{p}(V).

On the level of cohomology,
g_{p}
induces natural maps

H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V))\simeq H^{i}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V).

•

Greenberg’s conditions on
H^{1}
.

The conditions introduced here
are defined only on the level of the first cohomology group
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M).

Assume that
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Spm}(E),

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}=E
and
\mathcal{O}^{\circ}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{E}.

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a submodule of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V).

Whenever the map
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D})\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)
is injective, we denote by
H^{1}_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)

its image and set

H^{1}_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M):=\begin{cases}\mathrm{im}\left(H^{1}_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V/T)\right),&\textrm{if $M=V/T$},\\
\textrm{$\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)\longrightarrow\dfrac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}{H^{1}_{\mathbf{D}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}\right)$},&\textrm{if $M=T$}.\end{cases}

Bloch–Kato’s local conditions at
p
.

Here we assume that
\mathcal{X}=\mathrm{Spm}(E).

Following Bloch and Kato, we put

H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},M):=\begin{cases}\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathbf{B}_{\textrm{cris}})\right),&\textrm{if $M=V$},\\
\mathrm{im}\left(H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V/T)\right),&\textrm{if $M=V/T$},\\
\textrm{$\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\longrightarrow\dfrac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)}{H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)}\right)$},&\textrm{if $M=T$}.\end{cases}

1.2.2.
Selmer complexes

In this paper, we will work with Selmer complexes with coefficients in
V

defined by Greenberg local conditions arising from subrepresentations of
V
or, more generally,
submodules of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V).
Therefore our setting differs slightly from the point of view of
[
Nek06
]
, where Selmer complexes are defined on the integral level, and Pontryagin duality plays a central role.

We review general constructions following
[
Ben20
]
.

Let
V
be a
p
-adic representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}.

We will write
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V)
(resp.
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)
) for the complexes of continuous cochains
of
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
(resp. of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
) with coefficients in
V
.
Let

\textup{res}_{S}:=(\textup{res}_{\ell})_{\ell\in S}\,:\,C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)\xrightarrow{(\textup{res}_{\ell})_{\ell\in S}}\bigoplus_{\ell\in S}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},V)

denote the restriction map.
For any
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\},
set
K^{\bullet}_{\ell}(V)=C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},V)
and define
K^{\bullet}(V):=\underset{\ell\in S}{\bigoplus}K^{\bullet}_{\ell}(V)
. For each
\ell\in S
, let
f_{\ell}
denote the map given by

f_{\ell}=\begin{cases}\textup{res}_{\ell}&\text{if $\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}$},\\
\xi_{p}\circ\textup{res}_{p}&\text{if $\ell=p,$}\end{cases}

where
\xi_{p}
is the map from (
1.28
). We have a morphism of complexes:

f_{S}=(f_{\ell})_{\ell\in S}\,:\,C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)\longrightarrow K^{\bullet}(V).

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodule of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)
(not necessarily saturated). We associate to
\mathbf{D}
the collection
\left\{U_{\ell}^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}),g_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in S}
of local conditions given as follows:

U_{\ell}^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}):=\begin{cases}C^{\bullet}_{\textrm{ur}}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},V),&\forall\,\ell\in S\setminus\{p\},\\
C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D}),&\ell=p.\end{cases}

This data can be summarized with the diagram

(1.29)

\textstyle{C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{S}}
\textstyle{K^{\bullet}(V)}
\textstyle{U^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}),\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{S}}

where
U^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}):=\oplus_{\ell\in S}U^{\bullet}_{l}(V,\mathbf{D})
and
g_{S}:=(g_{\ell})_{\ell\in S}
.
Let
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V,\mathbf{D})=[U^{\bullet}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V,\mathbf{D})].
Then in the derived category
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules, the diagram (
1.29
) takes the form

\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{S}}
\textstyle{\underset{\ell\in S}{\oplus}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}
\textstyle{\underset{\ell\in S}{\oplus}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V,\mathbf{D}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{S}}

The Selmer complex associated to
\left\{U_{\ell}^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}),g_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in S}
is defined as

S^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}):=\mathrm{cone}\left[C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)\oplus U^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D})\xrightarrow{f_{S}-g_{S}}K^{\bullet}(V)\right][-1].

We denote by
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})=[S^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D})]
the corresponding object of the category of bounded perfect complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
-modules (see
[
Ben20
, Chapter 3]
for detail) and write

\mathbf{R}^{i}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})
for the cohomology of

S^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D}).
Note that
H^{i}(V,\mathbf{D})=0
for
i\geqslant 4.

Each cohomology class
[z_{\rm f}]\in H^{i}(V,\mathbf{D})
can be represented by a triple

z_{\rm f}=(z,(z_{\ell}^{+})_{\ell\in S},(\lambda_{\ell})_{\ell\in S}),

where

(1.30)

\displaystyle z\in C^{i}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V),

\displaystyle z_{\ell}^{+}\in U^{i}_{\ell}(V,\mathbf{D}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}\in K_{\ell}^{i-1}(V),

\displaystyle d(z)=0,

\displaystyle d(z_{\ell}^{+})=0,

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(z)=g_{\ell}(z_{\ell}^{+})-d(\lambda_{\ell}).

We will often write
(z,(z_{\ell}^{+}),(\lambda_{\ell}))
instead of

(z,(z_{\ell}^{+})_{\ell\in S},(\lambda_{\ell})_{\ell\in S})
.

In the setting of §
1.2.2
, we have a distinguished triangle

K^{\bullet}(V)\longrightarrow S^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D})[1]\longrightarrow\bigl(C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)\oplus U^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D})\bigr)[1]\longrightarrow K^{\bullet}(V)[1]\,,

which induces the long exact sequence

(1.31)

\begin{aligned} 0\longrightarrow&H^{0}(V,\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow H^{0}(\mathbb{Q},V)\oplus\left(\underset{\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}}{\bigoplus}H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)\oplus H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D})\right)\longrightarrow\underset{\ell\in S}{\bigoplus}H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)\\
&\longrightarrow H^{1}(V,\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)\oplus\left(\underset{\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}}{\bigoplus}H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)\oplus H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\mathbf{D})\right)\longrightarrow\underset{\ell\in S}{\bigoplus}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)\longrightarrow\cdots,\end{aligned}

1.2.3.
Selmer groups and Tate–Shafarevich groups

Throughout §
1.2.3
, we assume that
\mathcal{X}
is a point and
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}=E
. Suppose that
V
is a
p
-adic representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
with coefficients in
E
.

Let

U:=\{U_{\ell}(V)\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V),\ell\in S\}

be a family of local conditions defined on the level of cohomology groups in degree
1
.
Let us set
H^{1}_{S}(V):=H^{1}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V)
and put

H^{1}_{U}(V):=\ker\left(H^{1}(V)\rightarrow\underset{\ell\in S}{\bigoplus}\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}{U_{\ell}(V)}\right).

If
T
be a fixed lattice of
V,
we consider the local conditions on
T
and
V/T
given by

\displaystyle U_{\ell}(T):=\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)\to\dfrac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)}{U_{\ell}(V)}\right),

\displaystyle U_{\ell}(V/T):=\textrm{im}\left(U_{\ell}(V)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V/T)\right).

Using this data, we define the associated Selmer group and Tate–Shafarevich group on setting

\displaystyle H^{1}_{U}(V/T):=\ker\left(H^{1}(V/T)\rightarrow\underset{\ell\in S}{\bigoplus}\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V/T)}{U_{\ell}(V/T)}\right),

\displaystyle\Sha_{U}(T):=\mathrm{coker}\left(H^{1}_{U}(V)\rightarrow H^{1}_{U}(V/T)\right).

Here we record explicitly some important special cases of interest:

•

Bloch–Kato Selmer groups.
Here
U_{\ell}(V)=H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)
for all
\ell\in S.

The groups
H^{1}_{U}(V),

H^{1}_{U}(V/T)
and
\Sha_{U}(T)
coincide with
H^{1}_{\rm f}(V),

H^{1}_{\rm f}(V/T)
and
\Sha(T)
as defined by Bloch and Kato
[
BK90
]
.

•

Greenberg-style Selmer groups.
In this case,
U_{\ell}(V)=H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)
for all
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}
and

U_{p}(V)=\textrm{im}\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},Z)\rightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)\right),

where
Z
is either a
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-sub-representation of
V
or (more generally) a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodule
of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)
.

Replacing the local condition
U_{p}
at
p
with the entire local cohomology group at
p
, we obtain the
relaxed Selmer groups

H^{1}_{U,\{p\}}(V),

H^{1}_{U,\{p\}}(T)
and
H^{1}_{U,\{p\}}(V/T)
. Replacing the local condition at
p
with the zero subgroup, we obtain the
strict Selmer groups

H^{1}_{U,0}(V),

H^{1}_{U,0}(T)
and
H^{1}_{U,0}(V/T).

When
U
is the Bloch–Kato local conditions on
V
(so that
U_{\ell}(V)=H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)
for all
\ell\in S
), we write
H^{1}_{{\rm f},\{p\}}(V)
and
H^{1}_{0}(V)
to denote the corresponding relaxed and strict Selmer groups.

For each
\ell\in S,
let
U_{\ell}^{\perp}(V^{*}(1))\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V^{*}(1))
denote the orthogonal complement of
U_{\ell}(V)
under the local duality. The following proposition follows directly from the definitions:

Proposition 1.8
.

The following statements hold true:

The Poitou–Tate exact sequence induces the exact sequences

0\longrightarrow H^{1}_{U}(T)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{U,\{p\}}(T)\longrightarrow\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}{H^{1}_{U}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)}\longrightarrow\left(\frac{H^{1}_{U^{\perp}}(V^{*}/T^{*}(1))}{H^{1}_{U^{\perp},0}(V^{*}/T^{*}(1))}\right)^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0\,,

0\longrightarrow H^{1}_{U}(V)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{U,\{p\}}(V)\longrightarrow\frac{H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)}{H^{1}_{U}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V)}\longrightarrow\left(\frac{H^{1}_{U^{\perp}}(V^{*}(1))}{H^{1}_{U^{\perp},0}(V^{*}(1))}\right)^{\vee}\longrightarrow 0\,,

The Tate-Shafarevich group
\Sha_{U}(T)
is finite.

Proof.

The exact sequences in (i) follow from the Poitou–Tate exact sequence.
See
[
MR04
, Theorem 2.3.4]
or
[
Rub00
, Theorem I.7.3]
for details.
The finiteness of
\Sha_{U}(T)
is proved, for example, in
[
FPR94
, Section 5.3.5]
.
∎

1.2.4.
Selmer complexes in Perrin-Riou’s theory

Let
V
be a
p
-adic representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q},S}
with coefficients in
E
. We will assume that
V
is semistable at
p
and denote by
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)={\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{st}}(V)^{\varphi=N}
Fontaine’s modules associated to
V
. Let
T
be a
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-lattice of
V
stable under the Galois action.

We shall work in §
1.2.4
with Galois cohomology with coefficients in
V\otimes_{E}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)^{\iota}
, which can be viewed as the Iwasawa cohomology with coefficients in
V
. Set

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(T):=\mathbf{R\Gamma}(G_{S},T\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda^{\iota}),\qquad\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(V):=\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(T)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}E.

The cohomology of these complexes coincides with the classical Iwasawa cohomology

(1.32)

H^{i}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(T):=\underset{n}{\varprojlim}H^{i}(G_{\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{p^{n}})},T),\qquad H^{i}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(V):=H^{i}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(T)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}E.

Let
D
be a
\varphi
-submodule of
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)
. Let us set

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},D,V):=D\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda[-1]

and consider it together with the restriction of Perrin-Riou’s exponential map on
D\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda

(where we identify
\Lambda
with
\mathcal{O}[[X]]^{\psi=0}
):

\mathbf{Exp}_{V,h}\,:\,D\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda[-1]\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Lambda}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma).

This datum does not give rise to a local condition in the proper sense, but the induced
\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)
-linear map

\mathbf{Exp}_{V,h}\,:\,D\otimes_{E}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)[-1]\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},T)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Lambda}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)

defines a local condition at
p.
If
N\subset D
is an
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-lattice, then

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},N,T):=N\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda[-1]

is a
\Lambda
-lattice inside
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},D,V).

For all
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}
, take

(1.33)

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},N,T):=\left[T\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda^{\iota}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Fr}_{\ell}-1}T\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{E}}\Lambda^{\iota}\right].

The canonical map
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},N,T)\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)
induces a map

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},N,T)\otimes_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Lambda}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma),

which we consider as a local condition at
\ell.
These data can be summarized
by the diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(T)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\Lambda}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\underset{\ell\in S}{\oplus}\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)\otimes_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)}
\textstyle{\left(\underset{\ell\in S}{\oplus}\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},N,T)\right)\otimes_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\,.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The associated Selmer complex

(1.34)

\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw},h}(V,D):=\textrm{cone}\left[\left(\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(T)\oplus\left(\underset{\ell\in S}{\oplus}\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},N,T)\right)\right)\otimes_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\right.\\
\left.\longrightarrow\underset{\ell\in S}{\oplus}\mathbf{R\Gamma}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},T)\otimes_{\Lambda}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\right][-1]

plays a central role in Perrin-Riou’s theory.

1.2.5.
Pairings

We consider the following setting, which is a slight generalization of
the setting from
[
Ben20
, Chapter 3]
. Let
\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{w}\mathcal{W}

be a morphism of affinoids and let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
the associated
morphism of affinoid algebras. Let
V
and
V^{\prime}
be two Galois representations with coefficients in

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
equipped with
an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
-bilinear pairing

(1.35)

\left(\,,\,\right)\,\,:\,\,V^{\prime}\times V\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\,.

The pairing (
1.35
) induces a
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
-linear pairing

(\,,\,)\,\,:\,\,\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V^{\prime})\times\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\,.

We say that
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodules
\mathbf{D}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)
and

\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V^{\prime})
over
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{X}}
are orthogonal if

(x,y)=0,\qquad\forall\,\,x\in\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\quad\forall\,\,y\in\mathbf{D}.

Semi-local invariant maps

Recall the construction of semi-local invariant maps. For the proofs of the following facts and further details, we refer the reader to
[
Nek06
, Section 5.4.1]
(see also
[
Ben20
]
, Theorem 3.1.5). Consider the composition

\tau_{\geqslant 2}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))\xrightarrow{\textup{res}_{S}}\bigoplus_{\ell\in S}\tau_{\geqslant 2}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))\xrightarrow{f_{S}}\tau_{\geqslant 2}K^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1)),

where
\tau_{\geqslant 2}
denote the truncation at degree
2,

and define

E^{\bullet}=\mathrm{cone}\bigl[\tau_{\geqslant 2}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))\longrightarrow\tau_{\geqslant 2}K^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))\bigr][-1]\,.

The cohomology of
E^{\bullet}
is concentrated at degree
2.
More precisely, local class field theory gives rise to canonical isomorphisms
i_{\ell}\,:\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\simeq H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))
for each
\ell\in S
and we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\tau_{\geqslant 2}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{S}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\ell\in S}\tau_{\geqslant 2}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{E^{\bullet}[1]}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\ell\in S}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum}
\scriptstyle{(i_{\ell})_{\ell\in S}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{S}}

where
\sum
denotes the summation over
\ell\in S,
and
i_{S}=j\circ i_{\ell_{0}}
for some fixed
\ell_{0}\in S.
By global class field theory,
i_{S}
is a quasi-isomorphism, and there exists a homotopy inverse

r_{S}\,:\,E^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-3]

of
i_{S},
which is unique up to homotopy.

Assume that
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
are orthogonal. We then have a pairing

\cup^{r_{S}}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,S^{\bullet}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{A}S^{\bullet}(V,\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-3]

given as follows: If

y_{\rm f}=(y,(y_{\ell}^{+}),(\lambda_{\ell}))\in S^{i}(V^{\prime},\mathbf{D}),\quad z_{\rm f}=(z,(z_{\ell}^{+}),(\mu_{\ell}))\in S^{3-i}(V,\mathbf{D}),

then

\left(y_{\rm f}\right)\cup_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}^{r_{S}}\left(z_{\rm f}\right)=r_{S}\left(y\cup z,\lambda_{\ell}\cup f_{\ell}(z)+(-1)^{i}g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})\cup\mu_{\ell}\right).

Since the homotopy class of
r_{S}
does not depend on the choice of
r_{S},

it induces a canonical pairing

\cup_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-3]\,.

On the level of cohomology groups, this gives rise to the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
-linear pairings

\cup_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,{\mathbf{R}^{i}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes\mathbf{R}^{3-i}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}\,\,,\qquad 0\leqslant i\leqslant 3.

1.3.
p
-adic heights

1.3.1.
Heights afforded by cyclotomic variation

In this §
1.3.1
, we review the construction of
p
-adic heights. We refer the reader to
[
Nek06
,
Ben20
]
for further details.

The cyclotomic Galois group
\Gamma
has a canonical decomposition into the direct product
\Gamma=\Delta\times\Gamma_{1}.
Put
\Lambda(\Gamma_{1}):=\mathcal{O}_{E}[[\Gamma_{1}]]
and fix a generator
\gamma_{1}
of
\Gamma_{1}
. Recall that
\Lambda(\Gamma_{1})
is equipped with the
\mathcal{O}_{E}
-linear involution
\iota
given by
\iota(\gamma)=\gamma^{-1},

\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\textup{cyc}}=\Lambda(\Gamma_{1})^{\iota}/J^{2},
where
J:=\ker\left(\Lambda(\Gamma_{1})\xrightarrow{\gamma_{1}\mapsto 1}O_{E}\right)
denotes the augmentation ideal of
\Lambda(\Gamma_{1}),
and let
E^{\textup{cyc}}:=\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\textup{cyc}}[1/p].
There exists an exact sequence

(1.36)

0\longrightarrow E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{g}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E^{\textup{cyc}}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0

(which does not depend on the choice of
\gamma_{1}
), where
\mathfrak{g}(a)=a\tau
and
\tau=\dfrac{\gamma_{1}-1}{\log\chi(\gamma_{1})}
.

Let
V
be a
p
-adic Galois representation with coefficients in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
and let

\mathbf{D}
be a
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodule of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V).

Set
V^{\textup{cyc}}:=E^{\textup{cyc}}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}V
and
\mathbf{D}^{\textup{cyc}}=E^{\textup{cyc}}\widehat{\otimes}_{E}\mathbf{D}
.
The exact sequence (
1.36
) induces exact sequences

0\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow V^{\textup{cyc}}\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow 0\qquad,\qquad 0\longrightarrow\mathbf{D}\longrightarrow\mathbf{D}^{\textup{cyc}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{D}\longrightarrow 0.

This gives rise to a distinguished triangle of Selmer complexes

(1.37)

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\textup{cyc}},\mathbf{D}^{\textup{cyc}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\xrightarrow{\beta_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\textup{cyc}}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})[1]\,,

see
[
Ben20
, Section 3.2]
for a detailed exposition. The morphism
\beta_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\textup{cyc}}
is called the Bockstein morphism (for the infinitesimal cyclotomic variation).

We place ourselves in the scenario of Section
1.2.5
.
Let
(\,\,,\,\,)\,:\,V^{\prime}\times V\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
-bilinear pairing
and
\mathbf{D}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},A}(V)
and
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},A}(V^{\prime})
two
orthogonal
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodules.
The
p
-adic height pairing associated to these data is defined as the morphism

(1.38)

h^{\textup{cyc}}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\xrightarrow{\beta_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\textup{cyc}}\otimes\mathrm{id}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))[1]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\xrightarrow{\cup_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-2].

It induces an
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
-linear pairing on cohomology

\left<\,,\,\right>^{\textup{cyc}}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}.

For any
\kappa\in\mathcal{W}(E),
let
\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}
denote the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
associated to
\kappa.
Set
E_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}

and
\widetilde{E}_{\kappa}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}.

Set
V_{\kappa}=V\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}E_{\kappa}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}=\mathbf{D}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}E_{\kappa}.

Note that
V_{\kappa}
(respectively
\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}
) has a natural structure of
\widetilde{E}_{\kappa}
-module (respectively
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module over
\mathcal{R}_{\widetilde{E}_{\kappa}}
).

We record some functorial properties of Selmer complexes and
p
-adic heights in the following proposition:

Proposition 1.9
.

For any
\kappa\in\mathcal{W}
, one has

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))=\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\widetilde{E}_{\kappa},

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}E_{\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\cup_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}E_{\kappa}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\chi))\otimes_{E_{\kappa}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup_{\mathbf{D}_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{\kappa}}}
\textstyle{E_{\kappa}[-3].}

Assume that
\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V^{\prime})
are
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-submodules of
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V^{\prime}).
Then the canonical map

C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}(\chi))\rightarrow C^{\bullet}_{\varphi,\gamma}(\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\prime}(\chi))
induces a canonical map

\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}(\chi))\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\prime}(\chi)).

If
\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{D}\subseteq\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\mathcal{X}}(V)
are orthogonal, then
\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{D}
are orthogonal and
the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup_{\mathbf{D}_{1},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup_{\mathbf{D}_{2},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-3]}

\textstyle{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\textup{cyc}}_{\mathbf{D}_{1},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ \mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V,\mathbf{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\textup{cyc}}_{\mathbf{D}_{2},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}[-2].}

Proof.

The proof is purely formal and is omitted here.
∎

1.3.2.
Heights afforded by weight variation

The constructions in §
1.3.2
will not be used in our calculations with what we call the thick
p
-adic
L
-function in §
5
. However, one can prove that the
p
-adic heights afforded by weight variation (“weight-heights”) do appear in the formula for the second order derivative with respect to
X
of a two-variable
p
-adic
L
-function, whenever the relevant Bloch–Kato Selmer group is
1
-dimensional. We record the definition of the weight-height and review its basic properties for the sake of completeness.

We place ourselves in the situation of §
1.2.5
and §
1.3.1
. Let
\varpi_{\kappa}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}
be any generator of the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}
associated to
\kappa\in\mathcal{W}(E)
.

The short exact sequence

(1.39)

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}\xrightarrow{[\varpi_{\kappa}]}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}\longrightarrow 0

induces the short exact sequences

(1.40)

0\longrightarrow M_{\kappa}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[\varpi_{\kappa}]}}{{\longrightarrow}}M/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}M\longrightarrow M_{\kappa}\longrightarrow 0\,,\qquad M=V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)\,.

As in §
1.3.1
, these in turn give rise to a distinguished triangle

(1.41)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V^{\prime}(\chi)/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}&(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))\\
&\xrightarrow{\beta_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\rm wt}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))[1]\end{aligned}

where we call
\beta_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\rm wt}
the Bockstein morphism (for the infinitesimal weight variation). With this at hand, we define
h^{\rm wt}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}
as the compositum of the arrows

(1.42)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h^{\rm wt}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})\xrightarrow{\beta_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\rm wt}\otimes\mathrm{id}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))[1]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{W}}}^{\mathbf{L}}&\mathbf{R\Gamma}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})\\
&\quad\xrightarrow{\cup_{\mathbf{D}_{k},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{k}}}E[-2]\end{aligned}

mimicking the construction of
h^{\rm cyc}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}
in §
1.3.1
and denote by

\left<\,,\,\right>^{\rm wt}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}\,:\,{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))\times\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})}\longrightarrow E

the induced
E
-linear pairing.

1.3.3.
Rubin-style formulae

We compute (under a set of natural hypotheses) the height pairings we have introduced in §
1.3.1
and §
1.3.2
in terms of local duality pairings. This computation will play a crucial role in our leading term formulae in §
5.3
. Our computations are the generalizations of those Rubin proved in his much-studied works
[
Rub92
,
Rub94
]
and our treatment closely follows
[
Nek06
,
Büy16
,
BB23
]
.

In this subsection, let us denote by
W
(resp.
{\mathbb{D}}
) either
V
(resp. either
\mathbf{D}
) or
V^{\prime}(\chi)
(resp. or
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)
) to lighten our notation. Consider the exact sequence

(1.43)

0\longrightarrow W_{\kappa}\xrightarrow{[\varpi_{\kappa}]}W_{\epsilon}\xrightarrow{\pi_{\kappa}}W_{\kappa}\longrightarrow 0

where
W_{\epsilon}:=W/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}W
. We similarly put
{\mathbb{D}}_{\epsilon}:={\mathbb{D}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}{\mathbb{D}}
and define for
(Z,D)=(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}}_{k})
or
(W_{\epsilon},{\mathbb{D}}_{\epsilon})

\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(Z,{D})=\oplus_{\ell\in S}\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}_{\ell}(Z,{D}):={\rm cone}[U^{\bullet}(Z,D)\xrightarrow{-g_{S}}K^{\bullet}(Z)]\,.

Selmer complexes and singular quotients

For
(Z,D)=(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}}_{k})
or
(W_{\epsilon},{\mathbb{D}}_{\epsilon})
, we have the following tautological exact triangle in the derived category:

(1.44)

\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(Z,{D})[-1]\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}(Z,D)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{R\Gamma}(Z)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{S}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(Z,{D})\,,

where
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(Z)
(resp.
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(Z,D)
) denotes the image of the complex
C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},Z)
(resp. the complex
S^{\bullet}(Z,D)
given as in §
1.2.2
) in the derived category, cf.
[
Nek06
, Section 6.1.3]
. Moreover, for each
\ell\in S
, we have the tautological exact triangle

(1.45)

{U}_{\ell}^{\bullet}(Z,{D})\longrightarrow K_{\ell}^{\bullet}(Z)\longrightarrow\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}_{\ell}(Z,{D})\longrightarrow{U}_{\ell}^{\bullet}(Z,{D})[1]

in the derived category.

Set
\widetilde{D}:=\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(Z)/D
. Recall that
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},{D})
(resp.
\mathbf{R\Gamma}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{{D}})
) denotes the class of the complex
C_{\varphi,\gamma}^{\bullet}(D)

(resp.
C_{\varphi,\gamma}^{\bullet}(\widetilde{D})
) in the corresponding derived category (
D={\mathbb{D}}_{?}
with
?=\epsilon,\kappa
). We have a distinguished triangle

(1.46)

\mathbf{R\Gamma}({\mathbb{Q}_{p}},{D})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}({\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(Z))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{s}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{R\Gamma}({\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\widetilde{{D}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R\Gamma}({\mathbb{Q}_{p}},{D})[1]\,

The quasi-isomorphism
\alpha
in (
1.28
) together with the sequences (
1.45
) and (
1.46
) induce a functorial quasi-isomorphism

(1.47)

\mathbf{R\Gamma}({\mathbb{Q}_{p}},\widetilde{{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle qis}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{U}_{p}^{\bullet}(Z,{D})\,.

The composition of (
1.44
) and
(
1.47
) induces a map

(1.48)

\partial_{0}\,:\,H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{{D}})\longrightarrow\mathbf{R}^{1}\Gamma(Z,D)

which is compatible with all the duality statements in the obvious sense.

Bockstein-normalized derivative

The short exact sequence (
1.43
) induces a commutative diagram of complexes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{C}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\bullet}(W_{\epsilon},{\mathbb{D_{\epsilon}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{C}^{\bullet}(W_{\epsilon})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(W_{\epsilon},{\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{C}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

where
{C}^{\bullet}(Z)
is a shorthand for
{C}^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},Z)
for
(Z,D)=(W_{\kappa},\mathbb{D}_{k})
or
(W_{\epsilon},\mathbb{D}_{\epsilon})
. The horizontal (exact) lines are deduced from the defining properties of the Selmer complexes as mapping cones. On the level cohomology, this gives rise to the following commutative diagram with exact rows and columns:

(1.49)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 298.72565pt\raise-130.56006pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern Lemma 1.10
.

Suppose that we are given a class
[z_{\rm f}]=[(z,(z_{\ell}^{+})_{\ell\in S},(\lambda_{\ell})_{\ell\in S})]\in{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(W_{\kappa},\mathbb{D}_{\kappa})
such that

\pi_{\kappa}\left([\widetilde{z}]\right)=[z]\in{H^{1}(W_{\kappa})}

for some
[\widetilde{z}]\in{H^{1}_{S}(W)}
.
Then there exists a class

[D\widetilde{z}]=\left([D\widetilde{z}]_{\ell}\right)_{\ell\in S}\in\bigoplus_{\ell\in S}H^{1}(\widetilde{U}_{\ell}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}}))=H^{1}(\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}}))

such that

i([D\widetilde{z}])=\widetilde{\textup{res}}\left([\widetilde{z}]\mod\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}\right)\,.

\beta^{1}([z_{\rm f}])=-\partial([D\widetilde{z}])
.

Proof.

Since we have

\pi_{\kappa}\circ\widetilde{\textup{res}}\left([\widetilde{z}]\mod\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}\right)=\widetilde{\textup{res}}([z])=0

it follows, arguing as in Lemma 1.2.19 of
[
Nek06
]
, that there exists a class
d\in H^{1}(\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}}))
that verifies

•

i(d)=\widetilde{\textup{res}}\left([\widetilde{z}]\mod\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}^{2}\right)
,

•

\beta^{1}([z_{\rm f}])+\partial(d)-\partial\circ\beta^{0}(t)=0
for some
t\in H^{0}(\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}(W_{\kappa},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}}))
.

Set
[D\widetilde{z}]=d-\beta^{0}(t)
.
∎

Definition 1.11
.

In the situation of Lemma
1.10
, we let
\mathfrak{d}\left[\widetilde{z}\right]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}_{\kappa})
denote the image of
[{D}\widetilde{z}]_{p}
under the natural isomorphism
H^{1}(\widetilde{U}^{\bullet}_{p}(W_{\kappa},\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}))\xrightarrow{\eqref{eqn:identifyingsungularquotients}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbb{D}}_{\kappa})
.

We will call the class
\mathfrak{d}\left[\widetilde{z}\right]
the
Bockstein-normalized derived singular projection
of
\widetilde{z}
.

Rubin-style formula for weight-heights

The main goal in this subsection is to give a proof of the following Theorem, which we refer to as the
Rubin-style-formula
, where we calculate (under suitable hypotheses) the weight-height pairing

\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\kappa}^{\rm wt}:\qquad{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})}\xrightarrow{\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}^{\rm wt}}E

in terms of local duality pairings.

Theorem 1.12
.

Suppose that
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})=0
or, equivalently,
that
H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})=0
for all
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}
. Let
[y_{\rm f}]=[(y,(y_{\ell}^{+})_{\ell\in S},(\mu_{\ell})_{\ell\in S})]\in{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})
be any class. For
[z_{\rm f}]
and
[\widetilde{z}]
as in the statement of Lemma
1.10
(with
W=V^{\prime}(\chi)
) we have

\displaystyle\langle[z_{\rm f}],[y_{\rm f}]\rangle_{\kappa}^{\rm wt}

\displaystyle=-\textup{inv}_{p}\left((D\widetilde{z})_{p}\cup g_{p}(y_{p}^{+})\right)

\displaystyle=-\left<\mathfrak{d}\left[\widetilde{z}\right],\textup{res}_{p}([y])\right>

where
D\widetilde{z}\in{\widetilde{U}^{1}}(V^{\prime}_{\kappa}{(\chi)},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi)})
is any cocycle representing
[D\widetilde{z}]
.

See Lemma
1.14
below where we give a criterion for the vanishing of
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})
, which is useful in our applications in the context of the eigencurve.

Remark 1.13
.

As in
[
Büy16
, Remark A.3]
(appealing to the discussion
[
Ben20
, Section 1.2.1]
in place of
[
Nek06
]
, Sections 1.3.1 and 6.2.2), one may check for each coboundary
d(u,v)\in d\,\widetilde{U}_{\ell}^{0}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}{(\chi)},{\mathbb{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi)})
that

\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left(d(u,v)\cup{g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})}\right)=0

(even without assuming that
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})=0
) and therefore verify that the right side of the asserted equality in Theorem
1.12
is well-defined (despite the fact that
(D\widetilde{z})_{\ell}
is defined only up to a coboundary).

Proof of Theorem
1.12
.

This is entirely formal and follows the proof of
[
Nek06
, Proposition 11.3.15]
with obvious modifications.
Recall the complex

E^{\bullet}=\mathrm{cone}\left(\tau_{\geqslant 2}C^{\bullet}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},E(1))\xrightarrow{\textup{res}_{S}}\tau_{\geqslant 2}K^{\bullet}_{S}(E(1))\right)[-1].

Global class field theory gives rise to the following diagram with exact rows:

\textstyle{{H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{res}_{S}}
\textstyle{\oplus_{\ell\in S}{H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3}(E^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{inv}_{S}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{{H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{res}_{S}}
\textstyle{\oplus_{\ell\in S}{H^{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},\mathbb{Q}_{p}(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Suppose
[a_{\textup{f}}]=\left[(a,a_{S}^{+},\omega_{S})\right]\in{\mathbf{R}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))
and
[y_{\textup{f}}]=\left[(y,y_{S}^{+},\mu_{S})\right]\in{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})
, where we have set
a_{S}^{+}=(a_{\ell}^{+})
,
\omega_{S}=(\omega_{\ell})

y_{S}^{+}=(y_{\ell}^{+})
and
\mu_{S}=(\mu_{\ell})
to ease our notation. The formula

a_{\textup{f}}\,\widetilde{\cup}\,y_{\textup{f}}:=(a\cup y,\omega_{S}\cup\textup{res}_{S}(y)+g_{S}(a_{S}^{+})\cup\mu_{S})\in E^{3}

defines a cup product pairing

\widetilde{\cup}:S^{2}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes S^{1}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})\longrightarrow E^{3}\,.

This pairing is homotopic to the cup-product pairing given as in §
1.2.5
thanks to
[
Nek06
, Proposition 1.3.2]
. Therefore, the pairing on the level of cohomology

\langle\,,\,\rangle_{\textup{PT}}\,:\,{\mathbf{R}^{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))\otimes{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V_{\kappa},\mathbf{D}_{\kappa})\longrightarrow H^{3}(E^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\textup{inv}_{S}}E

(given by the discussion in §
1.2.5
) can be computed by the formula

\langle[a_{\textup{f}}],[y_{\textup{f}}]\rangle_{\textup{PT}}=\textup{inv}_{S}(a_{\textup{f}}\,\widetilde{\cup}\,y_{\textup{f}}).

Since the cohomological dimension of
G_{S}
equals to
2
,
a\cup y
is a coboundary
and there exists a cochain
C
such that
dC=a\cup y
. One may therefore compute
\textup{inv}_{S}([a_{\textup{f}}\,\widetilde{\cup}\,y_{\textup{f}}])
to be

\textup{inv}_{S}([a_{\textup{f}}\,\widetilde{\cup}\,y_{\textup{f}}])=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left(\omega_{\ell}\cup\textup{res}_{\ell}(y)+g_{p}(a_{\ell}^{+})\cup\mu_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(C)\right).

Let now
[a_{\textup{f}}]=\beta^{1}([z_{\textup{f}}]),
where
[z_{\textup{f}}]\in H^{1}(V_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\chi))
. Since
\pi_{\kappa}([\widetilde{z}])=[z],
it follows from the exact sequence

{H^{1}(V^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(\chi))\rightarrow H^{1}(V^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\chi))\xrightarrow{\beta^{1}}H^{2}(V^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\chi))}

that
a
is a coboundary. Write
a=dB
for some
B\in C^{1}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V^{\prime}_{\kappa})
, one may take
C=B\cup y
above. Then
g_{\ell}(a_{\ell}^{+})=d(\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))

for each
\ell\in S
and it is easy to check that

\beta^{1}\left([z_{\textup{f}}]\right)=\partial\circ\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{S}\left(\left[\left(\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B)\right)_{\ell}\right]\right).

Thence,

\displaystyle\langle[z_{\rm f}],[y_{\rm f}]\rangle_{\kappa}^{\rm wt}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left(\omega_{\ell}\cup\textup{res}_{\ell}(y)+g_{\ell}(a_{\ell}^{+})\cup\mu_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B)\cup\textup{res}_{\ell}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left((\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))\cup\textup{res}_{\ell}(y)+d(\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))\cup\mu_{\ell})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left((\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))\cup(\textup{res}_{\ell}(y)+d\mu_{\ell})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left((\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))\cup g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left(\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{\ell}(\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))\cup g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})\right).

We remark that
\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{\ell}(\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))
and
g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})
are cocycles for every
\ell
. Furthermore,

\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left(\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{\ell}(\omega_{\ell}+\textup{res}_{\ell}(B))\cup g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})\right)=0,\qquad\ell\neq p,

because
[g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})]\in H^{1}_{\textup{f}}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})=0
for
\ell\neq p
thanks to our running hypothesis. It follows from the definition of
(D\widetilde{z})_{p}
that

\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{p}(\omega_{p}+\textup{res}_{p}(B))=-(D\widetilde{z})_{p}+\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{p}(b)

for some
b\in C^{1}(G_{\mathbb{Q},S},V^{\prime}_{\kappa}).
An easy computation shows that

\textup{inv}_{p}(\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{p}(b)\cup g_{p}(y_{p}))=\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}(\widetilde{\textup{res}}_{\ell}(b)\cup g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}))=0\,;

cf. the proof of
[
Nek06
, Proposition 11.3.15]
. We now conclude that

\langle[z_{\rm f}],[y_{\rm f}]\rangle_{\kappa}^{\rm wt}=-\textup{inv}_{p}\left((D\widetilde{z})_{p}\cup g_{p}(y_{p}^{+})\right)

and the proof of our theorem is now complete.
∎

We close §
1.3.3
with a criterion for the vanishing of
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})
for
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}
. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}(E)
, let us put
V_{x}:=V\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}},x}E
and denote by
\mathfrak{m}_{x}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}
the maximal ideal corresponding
x
. For each such
x
, let us fix a generator
\varpi_{x}
of
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
.

Lemma 1.14
.

Let
\ell\in S\setminus\{p\}
be a prime and
\kappa\in\mathcal{W}(E)
. Suppose that
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{x})=0
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}(E)
that lies above
\kappa
. Then also
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})=0.

Proof.

Let
\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}\}\in\mathcal{X}(E)
denote the points above
\kappa
, so that we have

\mathfrak{m}_{\kappa}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathfrak{m}_{x_{1}}^{e_{1}}\cdots\mathfrak{m}_{x_{s}}^{e_{s}}\,,\qquad e_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\,.

Considering the
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-cohomology of the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{i}}^{j}V\xrightarrow{{[\varpi_{x_{i}}]}}V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{i}}^{j+1}V\longrightarrow V_{x_{i}}\longrightarrow 0

inductively (for
1\leq j\leq e_{i}-1
), together with our running assumption that

\dim H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{x_{i}})=\dim H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{x_{i}})=0\,,\qquad\forall i=1,\cdots,s\,,

we infer that

(1.50)

\dim H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{i}}^{e_{i}}V)=\dim H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{i}}^{e_{i}}V)=0\,,\qquad\forall i=1,\cdots,s\,.

Considering the
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}}
-cohomology of the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{1}}^{e_{1}}\cdots\mathfrak{m}_{x_{j-1}}^{e_{j-1}}V\xrightarrow{[\varpi_{x_{j}}^{e_{j}}]}V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{1}}^{e_{1}}\cdots\mathfrak{m}_{x_{j-1}}^{e_{j}}V\longrightarrow V/\mathfrak{m}_{x_{j}}^{e_{j}}V\longrightarrow 0

inductively (for
2\leq j\leq s
) together with (
1.50
), we deduce that

0=\dim H^{0}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})=\dim H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V_{\kappa})

as required.
∎

Rubin-style formula for cyclotomic heights

We record in this subsection the suitable variants of Theorem
1.12
for the height pairing
\left<\,,\,\right>^{\rm cyc}_{\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}}
. The notation of §
1.3.1
is in effect and we recall that
W
(resp.
{\mathbb{D}}
) denotes either
V
(resp. either
\mathbf{D}
) or
V^{\prime}(\chi)
(respectively, or
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)
). Analogous to (
1.49
), we have the following commutative diagram with exact rows and columns:

(1.51)

243.31703pt\raise-130.56006pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 243.31703pt\raise-130.56006pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
