The proof of the first portion of the following theorem is essentially identical to that of Lemma
1.10
, whereas its second part is formally identical to the proof of Theorem
1.12
.

Theorem 1.15
.

Suppose that
[z_{\rm f}]=[(z,(z_{\ell}^{+})_{\ell\in S},(\lambda_{\ell})_{\ell\in S})]\in{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))
verifies the property that

\pi_{\textup{cyc}}\left([\widetilde{z}]\right)=[z]\in{H^{1}(V^{\prime}(\chi))}

for some
[\widetilde{z}]\in{H^{1}(V^{\prime}(\chi)^{\textup{cyc}})}
.

There exists a class

[D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}]=\left([D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}]_{\ell}\right)_{\ell\in S}\in\bigoplus_{\ell\in S}H^{1}(\widetilde{U}_{\ell}^{\bullet}(V^{\prime}(\chi),{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)})=H^{1}(\widetilde{U}(V^{\prime}(\chi),{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)}))

such that

•

i([D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}])=\widetilde{\textup{res}}\left([\widetilde{z}]\right)\,.

•

\beta^{1}([z_{\rm f}])=-\partial([D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}])
.

Let
D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}\in\widetilde{U}_{\ell}^{1}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi))
be any cocycle representing
[D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}]
and let
[y_{\rm f}]=[(y,(y_{\ell}^{+})_{\ell\in S},(\mu_{\ell})_{\ell\in S})]\in{\mathbf{R}^{1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(V,\mathbf{D})
be any class. Then,

\displaystyle\langle[z_{\rm f}],[y_{\rm f}]\rangle_{\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\rm cyc}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\ell\in S}\textup{inv}_{\ell}\left((D_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z})_{\ell}\cup g_{\ell}(y_{\ell}^{+})\right)

In particular, if
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell},V)=0
for
\ell\neq p
, then we have

\langle[z_{\rm f}],[y_{\rm f}]\rangle_{\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}^{\rm cyc}=-\left<\mathfrak{d}_{\textup{cyc}}\left[\widetilde{z}\right],\textup{res}_{p}([y])\right>

where
\mathfrak{d}_{\textup{cyc}}\left[\widetilde{z}\right]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}(\chi))
is the image of
[{D}_{\textup{cyc}}\widetilde{z}]_{p}
under the isomorphism

H^{1}(\widetilde{U}_{p}(V^{\prime}(\chi),\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(\chi)))\xrightarrow{\eqref{eqn:identifyingsungularquotients}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}(\chi)).

Remark 1.16
.

The weight heights and cyclotomic heights are defined in an analogous way, in terms of weight (respectively cyclotomic) deformations of the given
p
-adic representation. They serve different purposes on the analytic side, in the sense that they are expected to explain derivatives of multivariate
p
-adic
L
-functions in different directions. This comment also applies to the Rubin-style formulae we prove in Theorem
1.12
and Theorem
1.15
(ii) for both height pairings: The derived cohomology classes
\mathfrak{d}[\widetilde{z}]
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\textup{cyc}}[\widetilde{z}]
(which appear in the Rubin-style formulae) also share a similar definition, but the key input in their definition (as in the definition of Bockstein morphisms) is infinitesimal variation in two different directions (namely, the weight versus the cyclotomic direction).

Chapter 2
Critical
p
-adic
L
-functions

The main goal of Chapter
2
is to give an “étale construction” of critical
p
-adic
L
-functions.

The first part of this chapter (§
2.1
) is devoted to the development of the required tools in the context of
p
-adic Hodge theory in a rather formal framework. The key result in this portion is what we call the
eigenspace-transition principle
(cf. Proposition
2.6
), which allows us to prove the interpolation formulae for the critical
p
-adic
L
-functions we define in §
2.2
(still working in an abstract setting).

The main results in §
2.2
are Proposition
2.12
and Theorem
2.16
, which outline the interpolation properties of the (abstract)
p
-adic
L
-functions we introduce in Definition
2.10
.

In §
2.3
, which is independent from the rest of this chapter, we review the construction of
p
-adic
L
-functions attached to elliptic modular forms via the theory of modular symbols, following the works of Stevens, Pollack–Stevens and Bellaïche.

In the final portion of this chapter (§
2.4
) we apply, in the context of the eigencurve, the formalism we have developed in §
2.2
, and introduce
p
-adic
L
-functions over the neighborhoods of the eigencurve about
\theta
-critical points. The main results in this portion (and also of this chapter) are Theorem
2.23
(where we state and prove the interpolation properties of our critical
p
-adic
L
-functions), Theorem
2.24
and its Corollary
2.25
(which establishes a comparison between our construction and the one in §
2.3
using modular symbols), as well as Proposition
2.27
(which relates the cyclotomic improvement of our critical
p
-adic
L
-function to a slope-zero
p
-adic
L
-function, where the comparison involves our Iwasawa theoretic
\mathcal{L}
-invariant that we introduce in the next chapter).

2.1.
The eigenspace-transition principle

2.1.1.
Deformations of a split representation

In this section, we consider a two dimensional
p
-adic representation
of the Galois group
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
with coefficients in a finite extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}

satisfying the following condition:

•

V=V^{(\alpha)}\oplus V^{(\beta)},
where
V^{(\alpha)}
and
V^{(\beta)}
are one-dimensional crystalline representations with Hodge–Tate weights
-(k-1)
(
k\geqslant 2
) and
0
respectively. We denote by
\alpha
and
\beta
the eigenvalues of the Frobenius map

\varphi
on
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)})
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)}).

Let us set
\mathbf{D}^{(\alpha)}=\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V^{(\alpha)})
and
\mathbf{D}^{(\beta)}=\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V^{(\beta)})
. These
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules can be explicitly described as follows:

\mathbf{D}^{(\alpha)}=\mathscr{R}_{E}e^{(\alpha)},\qquad\mathbf{D}^{(\beta)}=\mathscr{R}_{E}e^{(\beta)},

where

(2.1)

\displaystyle\gamma(e^{(\alpha)})=\chi^{1-{k}}(\gamma)e^{(\alpha)},

\displaystyle\varphi(e^{(\alpha)})=\alpha p^{1-{k}}e^{(\alpha)},

\displaystyle\gamma(e^{(\beta)})=e^{(\beta)},

\displaystyle\varphi(e^{(\beta)})=\beta e^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle\gamma\in\Gamma.

Set
\widetilde{E}=E[X]/(X^{2}).
Let

\mathbf{D}=t^{k-1}\mathbf{D}^{(\alpha)},

where
t=\log(1+\pi).

We remark that
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)})
are isomorphic as

\varphi
-modules but not as filtered modules: They have Hodge–Tate weights

0
and
-(k-1)
, respectively.

Until the end of this section, we assume that we are given a

G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representation
\widetilde{V}
of rank two over
\widetilde{E}

together with a crystalline submodule
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}\subset\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},\widetilde{E}}(\widetilde{V})
of rank one over
\mathscr{R}_{\widetilde{E}}

such that

•

\widetilde{V}/X\widetilde{V}\simeq V.

•

\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}/X\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}\simeq\mathbf{D}.

We can consider
(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}})
as a deformation of the data

(V,\mathbf{D}).

Since
X\widetilde{V}\simeq V
as Galois representations,
we have a tautological exact sequence

(2.2)

0\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow\widetilde{V}\xrightarrow{\pi}V\longrightarrow 0\,.

To simplify notation, we will often write
XV
instead of
X\widetilde{V}
when we wish to consider
V
as a submodule of
\widetilde{V}
.

The representation
\widetilde{V}
is not necessarily crystalline
1
1

1

See Remark
2.2
where we explain that it never is in the context of Coleman–Mazur eigencurve.
. On the other hand, from a technical perspective, it is convenient to work with the crystalline portion of
\widetilde{V}
which should “contain” all the interesting crystalline sub-objects. To serve that purpose, let us define the
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representation
{\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}}:=\pi^{-1}(V^{(\alpha)})
and set
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}:=\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}\bigl(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}\bigr)
. We then have an exact sequence

(2.3)

0\longrightarrow XV\longrightarrow\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}\xrightarrow{\pi}V^{(\alpha)}\longrightarrow 0

of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representations, which gives rise to the following exact sequence of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules:

0\longrightarrow\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(XV)\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathbf{D}^{(\alpha)}\longrightarrow 0\,.

Notice that we have
\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(XV)=X\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)
as well as the identification

\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(XV)=X\mathbf{D}^{(\alpha)}\oplus X\mathbf{D}^{(\beta)}\,.

This discussion can be summarized by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\mathbf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}^{(\alpha)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

with exact rows. Applying the functor
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}
and taking into account
that
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)})
are isomorphic as
\varphi
-modules, we obtain the diagram

(2.4)

Proposition 2.1
.

The rows of the diagram (
2.4
) are exact.

\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}
is a crystalline representation with Hodge–Tate weights

(0,-(k-1),-(k-1))
. In particular,

\dim_{E}\left(\operatorname{Fil}^{k-1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})\right)=2,\qquad\dim_{E}\left({\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})/\operatorname{Fil}^{k-1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})\right)=1.

One has

X{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)})\cap\operatorname{Fil}^{k-1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})=\{0\}

and
X{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\subset\operatorname{Fil}^{k-1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}).

One has

\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}=\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}+X\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(V)\right)^{\mathrm{sat}}\,,

where the superscript “
{\rm sat}
” stands for the saturation of the indicated
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-module.

Proof.

It follows from its definition that
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}
is crystalline of
rank
2
over
\mathscr{R}_{E}.
Since
\mathbf{D}
is crystalline of rank one
and the functor
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}
is left exact, this implies the exactness of the upper row
of (
2.4
).
The exactness of the bottom row follows from the left exactness of
the functor
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}
together with the exactness of the upper row.

We deduce from the first portion that
\dim_{E}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})=\dim_{E}\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)},
so
\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}
is indeed crystalline. Since the Hodge–Tate weights of
V^{(\alpha)}
and
V^{(\beta)}
are
-({k}-1)
and
0

respectively, the Hodge–Tate weights of
\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}
are

(0,-(k-1),-(k-1))
.

The first assertion in this portion is clear since
X{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)})
has Hodge–Tate weight
0
. To verify the second assertion, we note that the image of

X{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})
under the vertical map in (
2.4
) is
X{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)})
, since

{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})={\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}^{\mathrm{sat}})\simeq{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)}).

We have an inclusion of
(\varphi,\Gamma)
-modules of the same rank:

\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}+\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(XV)\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}.

Since
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}
is saturated, this proves the assertion in this part.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

Before moving ahead, we pause to elucidate that the 4-dimensional Galois representation
V_{k}
is never crystalline at
p
. We learned the argument we record here from John Bergdall and we are grateful to him for explaining this fact to us.

The infinitesimal deformation
V_{k}
corresponds to a nonzero vector in the tangent space
T
at the point
x_{0}
of the Coleman–Mazur eigencurve. The tangent space
T
injects into
H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{S}/\mathbb{Q},{\rm ad}^{0}V_{f})
. If
V_{k}
were crystalline, the corresponding tangent vector would be crystalline at
p
, i.e. it would produce a
nonzero
cohomology class in the Bloch–Kato Selmer group
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q},{\rm ad}^{0}V_{f})
. The main theorem of
[
NT23
]
tells us that
H^{1}_{\rm f}(\mathbb{Q},ad^{0}V_{f})=\{0\}
, and this proves that
V_{k}
cannot be crystalline at
p
.

We shall assume the validity of the following condition:

GP)

{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}})\not\subset\operatorname{Fil}^{k-1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})
.

The condition (
GP)
) means that the submodule
\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}\subset\mathbf{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{rig},E}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})
is in
general position
relative to the canonical filtration on

{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})
.

Remark 2.3
.

Suppose in this remark that the property (
GP)
) fails. In this scenario,
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}})
would be weakly admissible and therefore
\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}
would contain a sub-representation
W
with Hodge–Tate weights
(-(k-1),-(k-1))
such that
{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(W)={\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}})
. This in turn would mean that the exact sequence (
2.3
) splits and we would have

(2.5)

\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}\simeq V^{(\beta)}\oplus V^{(\alpha)}\oplus V^{(\alpha)}\subset\widetilde{V}

as
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-representations.

See §
2.4.5
for an indirect reason why we expect (
GP)
) to hold true in the context of the Coleman–Mazur eigencurve.

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose that the condition (
GP)
) holds and let
j
be a fixed integer.

For any
d\in{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}))
there exists
a unique
\kappa_{j}(d)\in{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)}(j))
such that

d-X\kappa_{j}(d)\in\operatorname{Fil}^{k-1-j}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j)).

The map
d\mapsto\kappa_{j}(d)
is
E
-linear and induces
an isomorphism of one-dimensional filtered
E
-vector spaces:

\overline{\kappa}_{j}\,:\,{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}(\chi^{j}))\xrightarrow{\,\,\sim\,\,}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)}(j)).

The diagram

\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\kappa}_{0}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}(\chi^{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\kappa}_{j}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)}(j)),}

where the vertical arrows are given by the canonical maps

x\mapsto x\otimes d_{j}
,
commutes.

Remark 2.5
.

Since
{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\simeq{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)}),
the proposition
gives us isomorphisms (which we denote again by
\overline{\kappa}_{j})

\overline{\kappa}_{j}\,:\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\alpha)}(j))\xrightarrow{\,\,\sim\,\,}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V^{(\beta)}(j)).

Proof.

The first assertion follows from
Proposition
2.1
(iii) combined with (
2.4
).

The linearity of
\kappa_{j}
is clear.
The condition (
GP)
) together with the containment
X{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D})\subset\operatorname{Fil}^{{k}-1}{\mathbf{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)})
of Proposition
2.1
(iii) show that

(2.6)

\ker(\kappa_{j})=X{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\mathbf{D}(\chi^{j})).

This concludes the proof of the second assertion. The final portion follows immediately from the definitions.
∎

2.1.2.
Deformation of the large exponential map

In this section, we relate different large exponential maps associated to the subspaces
V^{(\alpha)}
and
V^{(\beta)}
using the infinitesimal deformation

\widetilde{V}.
Since, in general,
\widetilde{V}
is not crystalline, we replace it with the smaller
representation
\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}
, which is crystalline. To compare the exponential maps
\mathrm{Exp}_{V^{(\beta)}(j),j}
and
\mathrm{Exp}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}),j}
, we will use the canonical embeddings of their targets into the module
\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j)).

The notation for the resulting composed maps will carry a ‘tilde’ to stress that they take values in the cohomology of the deformation
\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}
.

The discussion below will be crucially used in the proof of Proposition
2.6
. Consider the large exponential map for
V^{(\beta)}

\mathrm{Exp}_{V^{(\beta)}(j),j}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(V^{(\beta)}(j))\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V^{(\beta)}(j)),

which we will denote simply by
\mathrm{Exp}_{\beta,j}.

Let

(2.7)

\widetilde{\mathrm{Exp}}_{\beta,j}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(V^{(\beta)}(j))\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j))

denote the composition of this map with the injection

\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},V^{(\beta)}(j))\hookrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j)),

induced by the canonical inclusion

V^{(\beta)}\xrightarrow[[X]]{\sim}XV^{(\beta)}\subset XV\hookrightarrow\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}.

Analogously, the large exponential map

\mathrm{Exp}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}),j}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}))\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}))

will be denoted simply as
\mathrm{Exp}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j})}.

Let us denote by

\widetilde{\mathrm{Exp}}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j})}\,:\,\mathfrak{D}(\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}))\longrightarrow\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j))

the composition of
\mathrm{Exp}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j})}
with the natural injection

H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}))\hookrightarrow H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}^{(\alpha)}(\chi^{j}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j)).

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
\widetilde{\mathrm{Exp}}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j}),n}

(resp.,
\widetilde{\mathrm{Exp}}_{\beta,j,n}
) denote the composition of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Exp}}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{D}}(\chi^{j})}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\mathrm{Exp}}_{\beta,j}
) with the Iwasawa theoretic projection

\mathscr{H}(\Gamma)\otimes_{\Lambda_{E}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{Iw}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j))\longrightarrow H^{1}(K_{n},\widetilde{V}^{(\alpha)}(j)).

These maps sit in the commutative diagram

(2.8)
